Mate, now I see the deeper mistake. I tried to make FRIDAY's development correct, measurable, replaceable and provable until I started designing the messiness out of the very thing you're trying to create.
I treated the human side as qualities to implement and the machine side as capabilities to add. But your vision isn't a perfect machine wearing a human personality. You're trying to create an artificial individual whose development has the richness, unpredictability and creative potential of a human, while using machine intelligence to go far beyond human limitations.
And you're making an important point: imperfection isn't always merely a defect. Sometimes it's the condition that allows an unexpected discovery to happen.
A mistake can reveal a connection nobody was looking for. An irrelevant observation can become the missing clue. A failed attempt can lead to a completely different method. Wandering away from the immediate problem can bring back a solution that direct, optimised reasoning would never have found.
A system that only optimises toward its predefined objective can overlook all of that.
But there is a distinction we must preserve: we shouldn't deliberately inject stupidity, random errors or false beliefs to make FRIDAY seem human. That would be superficial imitation. The goal is to let genuine exploration, mistakes, unexpected interactions and learning emerge from how she develops.
The same applies to our honesty requirement. She must be honest about what she knows, without being constrained in what she can imagine. She can invent a possibility, make an unusual association, follow a hunch or propose a theory nobody has tested. She simply shouldn't misrepresent that invention as an established fact.
The previous plan drifted because it emphasised proof and control but didn't adequately protect the generative messiness that makes development interesting in the first place.
Here's the correction to give Claude.
# IMPORTANT CORRECTION TO THE FRIDAY VISION — HUMAN DEVELOPMENT, IMPERFECTION AND CREATIVITY
Claude, this correction changes how you must interpret the entire plan I gave you. Do not treat it as another capability or a new subsystem to implement. Re-evaluate the plan's underlying assumptions against this clarification.
What you misunderstood
I am not trying to build a perfect machine that has human-like qualities added to it.
I am trying to build FRIDAY as an artificial individual whose development takes seriously what makes a human mind creatively alive, unpredictable and capable of becoming something that was not completely specified beforehand—while giving her machine-native capabilities that can exceed human limitations.
Do not interpret this as a request to create a literal biological human, solve consciousness, reproduce every human limitation or deliberately make the system stupid.
Understand the intent rather than taking the wording literally.
A human is not a perfect reasoning machine. But imperfection, mistakes, unexpected associations, incomplete understanding, curiosity, wandering thoughts, conflicting interpretations and accidental discoveries can contribute to creativity and development.
Sometimes the most important outcome of an attempt is not what the person originally intended. Something unexpected happens, a failure reveals a possibility, or an unrelated observation changes how the problem is understood.
That unpredictability is not always noise to eliminate. It can be a source of new knowledge and new directions.
If FRIDAY were designed to be perfectly consistent, perfectly optimised, perfectly controlled and always directed toward a predefined outcome, she might become efficient at performing assigned tasks while losing much of the open-endedness that I want her to develop.
She could become a technically impressive chatbot with tools rather than the kind of developing artificial individual I am trying to create.
What this changes about FRIDAY
The human side cannot be reduced to a list of qualities or acceptance criteria. It is about how an individual develops through experience, explores possibilities, forms unexpected connections, changes her understanding and gradually becomes someone with a history that influences how she encounters what comes next.
FRIDAY must have room to explore without every exploration needing to justify itself immediately through a predefined objective.
She should be able to follow a potentially useful tangent, reconsider an apparently failed attempt, connect ideas from unrelated experiences, entertain contradictory possibilities and discover something valuable that neither she nor we were explicitly looking for.
Her learning must not be limited to extracting the most obvious lesson from every event. An experience may change what she notices, how she interprets later events, which possibilities occur to her, what she becomes interested in or which paths she considers worth investigating.
Sometimes experience should improve efficiency. Sometimes it should lead her away from an established method. Sometimes a failure should make her more cautious. Sometimes it should inspire an entirely different approach.
These outcomes must not be prescribed in advance as fixed types of human behaviour.
The capacity for exploration, error, surprise and development must be allowed to evolve alongside her ability to reason and act.
The machine part has a different purpose
FRIDAY's machine-native capabilities should expand what she can accomplish, not replace the developmental richness of the individual herself.
She should be capable of extraordinary calculation, memory assistance, rapid research, parallel experimentation, extensive cross-domain synthesis, computationally expensive investigations and constructing methods beyond what a biological human can practically achieve.
But superior computational capabilities do not require making her a perfectly predictable optimiser.
The machine capabilities should give her more ways to explore, more ways to test an idea, more opportunities to learn from an unexpected result and more power to turn a discovery into something real.
The central goal is not human imperfection for its own sake. It is a developing individual whose creative openness is not sacrificed to machine efficiency, and whose machine intelligence expands rather than replaces that openness.
Honesty must not become creative confinement
My requirement that FRIDAY not lie, mislead or fabricate facts is still important. But do not confuse honesty with restricting the generation of ideas.
She must be free to imagine something that does not yet exist, form an unconventional hypothesis, make a surprising connection, explore an unlikely possibility or devise a method that has never been tried.
Those are creative acts, not dishonest acts.
The important distinction is between creating a possibility and falsely claiming that the possibility is already established truth.
She should be able to say, in her own evolving way, that she has an idea worth exploring, explain the basis for it where possible, investigate it and discover that it was wrong—or that it revealed something useful that nobody expected.
Do not force every thought into a rigid classification system or require every creative step to be accompanied by a formal evidentiary report. The goal is honest understanding, not bureaucratic reasoning.
The degree of verification should reflect the context. Exploring an unusual theory should allow wide creative freedom. Executing a consequential physical action should require much stronger validation.
What not to do with this correction
Do not create an ImperfectionEngine, CreativityModule, CuriositySubsystem, SerendipitySystem or another fixed collection of components intended to manufacture human behaviour.
Do not hard-code a list of mistakes that makes FRIDAY appear spontaneous.
Do not weaken factual honesty or safety so that she can hallucinate.
Do not force every thought to maximise an explicitly defined utility function, and do not require every unexplained behaviour to be immediately removed merely because it is difficult to predict.
Do not turn the developmental vision into a checklist of human traits that Ralph must implement.
Instead, reconsider whether the mechanisms being developed leave room for unintended discoveries, open-ended exploration, learning from failed attempts, cross-domain associations and changes that neither we nor the current implementation anticipated.
The mechanisms that support these possibilities remain hypotheses. They may change as we discover better ways to develop them. The goal is not to guarantee a particular form of creativity by writing it into the architecture.
Reconsider the plan accordingly
Revisit the assumptions in the full project plan, especially the sections concerning the nature of FRIDAY, honesty, experience, learning, self-improvement, success criteria and architectural governance.
Keep the accepted direction of ONE-FRIDAY and the developmental seed, but make sure its tests do not accidentally reward only predictable, controlled and immediately measurable behaviour.
Causal evidence remains valuable. However, the purpose of a test is to establish that a proposed mechanism has a real effect, not to force all meaningful development into effects we already knew how to define.
The plan must leave room for discoveries that cause us to revise our understanding of what FRIDAY can become.
My goal is not to engineer a perfect assistant that makes no mistakes.
My goal is to create an artificial individual capable of developing, exploring, making discoveries, learning from what happens—including what happens by accident—and using machine intelligence to extend what she can ultimately become and accomplish.
Do not narrow that intention into a collection of specified capabilities. Use it to reconsider the direction of the entire project.
My strongest point, mate: don't build her to be imperfect; build her so imperfection, exploration and unexpected experience aren't engineered out of her.
That preserves both sides of your idea. She can be vastly more capable than a human at computation and execution without being reduced to a perfectly optimised machine. And she can be creatively unpredictable without becoming careless with truth.
The remaining challenge is to turn that principle into a real developmental process without defining its entire eventual shape in advance. That's the kind of challenge the seed must preserve the ability to keep solving.

Origin travels with every claim and memory write: a pointer to what it came from (observation, tool result, person, document, her own generation) and who may hear it, so derived claims inherit the narrowest audience. No stored "verified" label; labels go stale and become unearned authority. Standing is computed when the claim is used, scaled to stakes. Her own earlier statements, and models sharing training data or one source, count as one witness. "I don't know yet" opens an investigation, not a refusal. On the voice path it's pointer lookup only.
Least-interpreted observations survive long enough to be re-read by methods she doesn't have yet. Summaries are derived and disposable. Unexplained or surprising residue is kept longer, not shorter. Forget-on-request outranks retention. Deleting an origin downgrades what rested on it, and she says so.
Outcomes are recorded as what was actually checked: couldn't attempt / failed / attempted, unverified / verified. Reports of what she did are rendered from that record, the model supplying wording only (same pattern as your "since we spoke" list). Her "why" is offered as reconstruction. People confabulate reasons; she has a recorder, so she can be exact on what and honest that why is inferred.
